I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there anxious for no reason out of nowhere. It’s more tied to coming home and dealing with the situation there.\n\nLike, at work, I can still function. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on shift I kind of go into that mode where there’s a job to do and I do it. I’m not walking around panicking or unable to calm down. But once I’m home, with my father-in-law living with us now, and then trying to keep up with coursework too, I feel more wound up than I’m used to. I get short faster. I don’t settle the way I normally would.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help with that — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So it’s not like I’m constantly on edge for no reason, but I’m definitely carrying more stress around at home than feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of home because of your father-in-law's presence?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am, at least more than I used to. I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid of seeing them or anything like that. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in, home has gotten tense enough that I feel like I’m using up all my patience just getting through the day.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together. I’m an EMT, so when I’m there, I’ve got a role and a structure and I just do what needs doing. But then I get home, and there’s the situation with him in the house, and then I’ve still got coursework hanging over me, and I’m just more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nSo when something comes up outside the house — even things I normally like, like fishing, woodworking, or those first-aid workshops I’d usually go to — I end up skipping them. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, exactly. They’re actually the things that usually keep my head clear. But lately I just don’t have the mental room for them, or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're enjoying the things you're still doing, like your work as an EMT, less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I’d put it a little differently. When I’m actually working as an EMT, I can still do the job. I still care about doing it right, and when there’s a call or something needs handling, I can get into that mode and focus. So it’s not like I suddenly hate my work or feel nothing about it.\n\nBut I think I have less room in me, if that makes sense. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to hold it together at work, but then at home — and with my coursework on top of it — I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. So by the time I’m off shift, the things that usually help me feel like myself, like fishing or woodworking or even the first-aid workshops I normally like, I’ve been skipping those.\n\nSo with work, I’m still functioning and I don’t think I’ve lost all enjoyment of it. But I’m not getting as much satisfaction from the rest of my life around it, because I’m just carrying all this stress and not doing the things that usually clear my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed by your emotions most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say most of the time. I don’t feel like I’m falling apart all day long or anything. I’m still holding it together at work, and with EMT work you kind of have to — there’s not a lot of room to get wrapped up in yourself when something needs doing.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in with us, yeah, I’ve felt more overwhelmed than I’m used to, especially once I’m home. It’s like I can keep everything contained during the shift, and then I get back to the house and there’s the tension there, and then I’m also trying to keep up with coursework, and I just feel wound tighter. I get short more easily, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed I’m not doing the things that usually help me reset. Fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — those are usually the things that keep my head clear and make me feel like I’ve got some balance. Lately I’ve been skipping them, which probably makes the whole thing worse. So… not overwhelmed by my emotions every minute, no, but at home it’s definitely been more than I’m used to carrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about your living situation since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it’s not like I’m sitting there every second thinking, “What are we going to do about the house?” It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, it’s kind of become the background noise in my head.\n\nI can still keep it together at work. When I’m on as an EMT, I know what I’m supposed to do, and I can stay focused on the call or the patient or whatever’s in front of me. But once I’m home, or even when I’m trying to do coursework, that tension in the living situation is right there again. I feel more wound up than I’m used to, and I get short more easily, which I don’t like.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me is I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are normally how I clear my head. Lately I just skip them because I feel like I don’t have the space for anything else. So, yeah, I’d say I’m pretty preoccupied with it, especially outside of work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I’d say it is interfering, though maybe not in the way people might picture, like I’m completely unable to function. I’m still doing my EMT shifts. When I’m there, I show up, I do the work, and I can stay focused enough to handle what needs handling.

I’ll sit down meaning to catch up, and then I just don’t have the bandwidth, or I put it off because I feel wound up about everything at home.

And I’ve also stopped showing up consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those used to be something I cared about and made time for, and lately I’ve been missing them or not committing the way I normally would. So… yes, I’m still functioning in the big obvious sense, but it’s getting in the way of my daily life, especially with the coursework and the volunteer-type stuff I normally keep up with.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thanks. I guess I’m here because I’m not quite feeling like myself lately. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been managing okay at work — I’m an EMT, so I can kind of compartmentalize and do what needs doing when I’m on shift — but when I get home, or when I’m trying to keep up with my coursework, I’m just more wound up than usual.\n\nI’m shorter with people than I want to be, more on edge, and it’s not really how I usually operate. I’m still doing my job, and honestly at work I can keep it together pretty well because I have to — being an EMT, you kind of switch into that mode and just handle what’s in front of you.\n\nBut then I get home, and instead of feeling like I can reset, I’m already tense. And then I’ve got coursework sitting there too, so it feels like there’s no clean break. I notice I’m more wound up, more short with people than I’m used to. So then the things that normally help me clear my head — going fishing, spending time woodworking, even going to first-aid workshops — I just start skipping them. Not because I don’t like them anymore, but because I feel like I don’t have the mental room for them, even though I know they’d probably help.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as sad or down most of the time, no… or at least I haven’t really thought of it that way. It’s more like I notice something’s off, if that makes sense, but I don’t know that I’d label it as sadness.\n\nThere are moments where I feel kind of discouraged or just tired of the situation, but “sad most of the time” feels stronger than what I’ve noticed. I guess I’m still trying to sort out exactly what I’m feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m worrying excessively about every little everyday thing, exactly. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m carrying this constant tension around, especially once I’m home. At work, I can still hold it together — as an EMT, I’m used to compartmentalizing and just doing what has to be done.\n\nBut when I get home, and then I’m trying to deal with coursework too, I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I get short more easily, and instead of doing the things that usually clear my head — fishing, woodworking, even first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping them. So it’s not like I’m sitting there worrying about bills or errands or every small thing all day, but the stress around the home situation is definitely spilling into the rest of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have difficulty relaxing since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think “difficulty relaxing” fits pretty well. Restless maybe not in the sense that I’m pacing around constantly, but since my father-in-law moved in, I feel like I’m wound tighter than I used to be, especially at home.\n\nAt work I can still function — I mean, as an EMT you kind of have to stay focused and keep things moving — but once I get home, I don’t really settle. And then I’ve got coursework to deal with too, so it feels like I’m going from one pressure into another without much of a break. I notice I’m shorter than usual, like I don’t have as much patience.\n\nAnd the weird thing is, the stuff that usually helps me relax — fishing, woodworking, even going to first-aid workshops — I’ve started skipping. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, but because by the time I could do them, I feel like I don’t have the headspace. So yeah, I guess I have been having a harder time relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about your father-in-law that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, there’s a particular memory of him that I’m pushing away or some specific thought that pops up and I have to avoid it.\n\nI’m trying to think about that honestly. It’s not like I’m having flashbacks or replaying one certain conversation with him over and over. Mostly when I think about him, it’s more just the general situation of him being in the house and everything feeling more crowded or tense. But I can’t point to one specific memory and say, “That’s what I’m avoiding.”\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that. If I’m avoiding anything, it’s probably more the overall tension than a specific thought about him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're anxious most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious most of the time, exactly. It’s more like… since my father-in-law moved in, I’m carrying this stress around that I can’t quite put down once I’m home.\n\nAt work, I’m still able to hold it together. Being an EMT, I can kind of switch into that mode where I know what needs doing and I do it. But then I get home, and there’s the situation in the house, and then I’ve got coursework on top of that, and I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I notice I’m shorter with people, less patient.\n\nAnd I’ve started skipping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like going to. So I don’t know if I’d call it being anxious all the time, but I’m definitely more tense and not myself since he moved in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in the things you normally enjoy doing since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, although I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly. It’s more like I still know those things matter to me, but I’m not doing them.\n\nFishing used to be one of the main ways I’d clear my head. Same with woodworking — just having something in front of me where I could focus on measuring, sanding, fixing something with my hands. And I’ve always liked going to first-aid workshops too, even with being an EMT, because it felt useful and kind of grounding in a weird way.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m holding it together at work, and then when I get home there’s all the tension there, and then I’ve got coursework sitting on top of that. By the time I could go do something for myself, I’m already wound up or short-tempered or just mentally done. So I end up skipping it.\n\nSo yes, I’m doing those things less. I don’t think I stopped caring about them, but I’ve definitely pulled back from the things that normally help keep my head clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything — I’m not someone who weighs myself every morning — but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone mention that I look different.\n\nWith all the stress at home, I guess I could see how someone might eat more or less, but I haven’t noticed a big change that way. Maybe my eating is a little less organized some days, just because the house feels off and I’m not always in the mood to sit down for a normal meal, but in terms of actual weight… no, nothing significant that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on edge and can't calm down, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, no. I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there anxious for no reason out of nowhere. It’s more tied to coming home and dealing with the situation there.\n\nLike, at work, I can still function. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on shift I kind of go into that mode where there’s a job to do and I do it. I’m not walking around panicking or unable to calm down. But once I’m home, with my father-in-law living with us now, and then trying to keep up with coursework too, I feel more wound up than I’m used to. I get short faster. I don’t settle the way I normally would.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help with that — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So it’s not like I’m constantly on edge for no reason, but I’m definitely carrying more stress around at home than feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of home because of your father-in-law's presence?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am, at least more than I used to. I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid of seeing them or anything like that. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in, home has gotten tense enough that I feel like I’m using up all my patience just getting through the day.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together. I’m an EMT, so when I’m there, I’ve got a role and a structure and I just do what needs doing. But then I get home, and there’s the situation with him in the house, and then I’ve still got coursework hanging over me, and I’m just more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nSo when something comes up outside the house — even things I normally like, like fishing, woodworking, or those first-aid workshops I’d usually go to — I end up skipping them. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, exactly. They’re actually the things that usually keep my head clear. But lately I just don’t have the mental room for them, or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're enjoying the things you're still doing, like your work as an EMT, less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I’d put it a little differently. When I’m actually working as an EMT, I can still do the job. I still care about doing it right, and when there’s a call or something needs handling, I can get into that mode and focus. So it’s not like I suddenly hate my work or feel nothing about it.\n\nBut I think I have less room in me, if that makes sense. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to hold it together at work, but then at home — and with my coursework on top of it — I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. So by the time I’m off shift, the things that usually help me feel like myself, like fishing or woodworking or even the first-aid workshops I normally like, I’ve been skipping those.\n\nSo with work, I’m still functioning and I don’t think I’ve lost all enjoyment of it. But I’m not getting as much satisfaction from the rest of my life around it, because I’m just carrying all this stress and not doing the things that usually clear my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed by your emotions most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say most of the time. I don’t feel like I’m falling apart all day long or anything. I’m still holding it together at work, and with EMT work you kind of have to — there’s not a lot of room to get wrapped up in yourself when something needs doing.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in with us, yeah, I’ve felt more overwhelmed than I’m used to, especially once I’m home. It’s like I can keep everything contained during the shift, and then I get back to the house and there’s the tension there, and then I’m also trying to keep up with coursework, and I just feel wound tighter. I get short more easily, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed I’m not doing the things that usually help me reset. Fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — those are usually the things that keep my head clear and make me feel like I’ve got some balance. Lately I’ve been skipping them, which probably makes the whole thing worse. So… not overwhelmed by my emotions every minute, no, but at home it’s definitely been more than I’m used to carrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about your living situation since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it’s not like I’m sitting there every second thinking, “What are we going to do about the house?” It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, it’s kind of become the background noise in my head.\n\nI can still keep it together at work. When I’m on as an EMT, I know what I’m supposed to do, and I can stay focused on the call or the patient or whatever’s in front of me. But once I’m home, or even when I’m trying to do coursework, that tension in the living situation is right there again. I feel more wound up than I’m used to, and I get short more easily, which I don’t like.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me is I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are normally how I clear my head. Lately I just skip them because I feel like I don’t have the space for anything else. So, yeah, I’d say I’m pretty preoccupied with it, especially outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are experiencing anxiety that interferes with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it is interfering, though maybe not in the way people might picture, like I’m completely unable to function. I’m still doing my EMT shifts. When I’m there, I show up, I do the work, and I can stay focused enough to handle what needs handling.\n\nBut outside of that, it’s definitely started to affect things. I’ve fallen behind on some of my paramedic coursework, which bothers me because that’s not really how I usually operate. I’ll sit down meaning to catch up, and then I just don’t have the bandwidth, or I put it off because I feel wound up about everything at home.\n\nAnd I’ve also stopped showing up consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those used to be something I cared about and made time for, and lately I’ve been missing them or not committing the way I normally would. So… yes, I’m still functioning in the big obvious sense, but it’s getting in the way of my daily life, especially with the coursework and the volunteer-type stuff I normally keep up with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not one specific blowup, no… at least not one I can point to and say, “That was the moment.” It’s more been the whole adjustment since my father-in-law moved in with us. The house just feels different now, and I’ve been trying to keep my head down and keep functioning.

I haven’t been going fishing much, I’m not doing woodworking like I usually do, and I’ve skipped some first-aid workshops too, which is unusual for me because those things usually help keep my head clear. I figured I should come talk to someone before it keeps building.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of your father-in-law moving in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, though it’s not like I’m avoiding the house exactly — I still go home, I still do what I need to do. But since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve definitely started backing away from the things that usually help me decompress. Fishing, woodworking in the garage, even those first-aid workshops I’d normally make time for… I’ve been skipping them more.\n\nPart of it is that I get home and I’m already wound up, and then with coursework on top of everything, I just sort of shut down or get short instead of doing the stuff that would probably clear my head. At work I can hold it together pretty well, because that’s just what you do as an EMT, but outside of work I can tell I’m not handling it the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your father-in-law moving in that's causing you to avoid those activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the specific thing is less one dramatic incident and more that since he moved in, home doesn’t feel like the place where I can really come down from everything anymore. I’m still doing my job, and honestly at work I can keep it together pretty well because I have to — being an EMT, you kind of switch into that mode and just handle what’s in front of you.\n\nBut then I get home, and instead of feeling like I can reset, I’m already tense. And then I’ve got coursework sitting there too, so it feels like there’s no clean break. I notice I’m more wound up, more short with people than I’m used to. So then the things that normally help me clear my head — going fishing, spending time woodworking, even going to first-aid workshops — I just start skipping them. Not because I don’t like them anymore, but because I feel like I don’t have the mental room for them, even though I know they’d probably help.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as sad or down most of the time, no… or at least I haven’t really thought of it that way. It’s more like I notice something’s off, if that makes sense, but I don’t know that I’d label it as sadness.\n\nThere are moments where I feel kind of discouraged or just tired of the situation, but “sad most of the time” feels stronger than what I’ve noticed. I guess I’m still trying to sort out exactly what I’m feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m worrying excessively about every little everyday thing, exactly. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m carrying this constant tension around, especially once I’m home. At work, I can still hold it together — as an EMT, I’m used to compartmentalizing and just doing what has to be done.\n\nBut when I get home, and then I’m trying to deal with coursework too, I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I get short more easily, and instead of doing the things that usually clear my head — fishing, woodworking, even first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping them. So it’s not like I’m sitting there worrying about bills or errands or every small thing all day, but the stress around the home situation is definitely spilling into the rest of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have difficulty relaxing since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think “difficulty relaxing” fits pretty well. Restless maybe not in the sense that I’m pacing around constantly, but since my father-in-law moved in, I feel like I’m wound tighter than I used to be, especially at home.\n\nAt work I can still function — I mean, as an EMT you kind of have to stay focused and keep things moving — but once I get home, I don’t really settle. And then I’ve got coursework to deal with too, so it feels like I’m going from one pressure into another without much of a break. I notice I’m shorter than usual, like I don’t have as much patience.\n\nAnd the weird thing is, the stuff that usually helps me relax — fishing, woodworking, even going to first-aid workshops — I’ve started skipping. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, but because by the time I could do them, I feel like I don’t have the headspace. So yeah, I guess I have been having a harder time relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about your father-in-law that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, there’s a particular memory of him that I’m pushing away or some specific thought that pops up and I have to avoid it.\n\nI’m trying to think about that honestly. It’s not like I’m having flashbacks or replaying one certain conversation with him over and over. Mostly when I think about him, it’s more just the general situation of him being in the house and everything feeling more crowded or tense. But I can’t point to one specific memory and say, “That’s what I’m avoiding.”\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that. If I’m avoiding anything, it’s probably more the overall tension than a specific thought about him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're anxious most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious most of the time, exactly. It’s more like… since my father-in-law moved in, I’m carrying this stress around that I can’t quite put down once I’m home.\n\nAt work, I’m still able to hold it together. Being an EMT, I can kind of switch into that mode where I know what needs doing and I do it. But then I get home, and there’s the situation in the house, and then I’ve got coursework on top of that, and I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I notice I’m shorter with people, less patient.\n\nAnd I’ve started skipping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like going to. So I don’t know if I’d call it being anxious all the time, but I’m definitely more tense and not myself since he moved in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in the things you normally enjoy doing since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, although I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly. It’s more like I still know those things matter to me, but I’m not doing them.\n\nFishing used to be one of the main ways I’d clear my head. Same with woodworking — just having something in front of me where I could focus on measuring, sanding, fixing something with my hands. And I’ve always liked going to first-aid workshops too, even with being an EMT, because it felt useful and kind of grounding in a weird way.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m holding it together at work, and then when I get home there’s all the tension there, and then I’ve got coursework sitting on top of that. By the time I could go do something for myself, I’m already wound up or short-tempered or just mentally done. So I end up skipping it.\n\nSo yes, I’m doing those things less. I don’t think I stopped caring about them, but I’ve definitely pulled back from the things that normally help keep my head clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything — I’m not someone who weighs myself every morning — but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone mention that I look different.\n\nWith all the stress at home, I guess I could see how someone might eat more or less, but I haven’t noticed a big change that way. Maybe my eating is a little less organized some days, just because the house feels off and I’m not always in the mood to sit down for a normal meal, but in terms of actual weight… no, nothing significant that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on edge and can't calm down, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, no. I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there anxious for no reason out of nowhere. It’s more tied to coming home and dealing with the situation there.\n\nLike, at work, I can still function. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on shift I kind of go into that mode where there’s a job to do and I do it. I’m not walking around panicking or unable to calm down. But once I’m home, with my father-in-law living with us now, and then trying to keep up with coursework too, I feel more wound up than I’m used to. I get short faster. I don’t settle the way I normally would.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help with that — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So it’s not like I’m constantly on edge for no reason, but I’m definitely carrying more stress around at home than feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of home because of your father-in-law's presence?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am, at least more than I used to. I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid of seeing them or anything like that. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in, home has gotten tense enough that I feel like I’m using up all my patience just getting through the day.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together. I’m an EMT, so when I’m there, I’ve got a role and a structure and I just do what needs doing. But then I get home, and there’s the situation with him in the house, and then I’ve still got coursework hanging over me, and I’m just more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nSo when something comes up outside the house — even things I normally like, like fishing, woodworking, or those first-aid workshops I’d usually go to — I end up skipping them. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, exactly. They’re actually the things that usually keep my head clear. But lately I just don’t have the mental room for them, or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're enjoying the things you're still doing, like your work as an EMT, less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I’d put it a little differently. When I’m actually working as an EMT, I can still do the job. I still care about doing it right, and when there’s a call or something needs handling, I can get into that mode and focus. So it’s not like I suddenly hate my work or feel nothing about it.\n\nBut I think I have less room in me, if that makes sense. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to hold it together at work, but then at home — and with my coursework on top of it — I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. So by the time I’m off shift, the things that usually help me feel like myself, like fishing or woodworking or even the first-aid workshops I normally like, I’ve been skipping those.\n\nSo with work, I’m still functioning and I don’t think I’ve lost all enjoyment of it. But I’m not getting as much satisfaction from the rest of my life around it, because I’m just carrying all this stress and not doing the things that usually clear my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed by your emotions most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say most of the time. I don’t feel like I’m falling apart all day long or anything. I’m still holding it together at work, and with EMT work you kind of have to — there’s not a lot of room to get wrapped up in yourself when something needs doing.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in with us, yeah, I’ve felt more overwhelmed than I’m used to, especially once I’m home. It’s like I can keep everything contained during the shift, and then I get back to the house and there’s the tension there, and then I’m also trying to keep up with coursework, and I just feel wound tighter. Fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — those are usually the things that keep my head clear and make me feel like I’ve got some balance. Lately I’ve been skipping them, which probably makes the whole thing worse. So… not overwhelmed by my emotions every minute, no, but at home it’s definitely been more than I’m used to carrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about your living situation since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it’s not like I’m sitting there every second thinking, “What are we going to do about the house?” It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, it’s kind of become the background noise in my head.\n\nI can still keep it together at work. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so… though it’s not always just one category like finances or chores exactly. It’s more like since my father-in-law moved in, the whole household feels harder to relax in, and little everyday things get under my skin more than they used to.

I mean, I came in because I’ve noticed I can still hold it together at work — when I’m on shift as an EMT, I can stay focused and do what I need to do. But then I get home, or I sit down to work on my coursework, and I’m just more wound up. I’ll be shorter with people than I want to be, or I’ll feel like I can’t settle into anything.

And the part that really tells me something is off is that I’ve started skipping the stuff that normally helps me clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are usually the things that keep me balanced, and lately I either don’t go or don’t even make plans for them. So yeah, I’d say I’m worrying and carrying the household stress around more than I used to, even if it’s not always about one specific chore or bill.

I get short more easily, and instead of doing the things that usually clear my head — fishing, woodworking, even first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping them. And then I’ve got coursework to deal with too, so it feels like I’m going from one pressure into another without much of a break. I notice I’m shorter than usual, like I don’t have as much patience.\n\nAnd the weird thing is, the stuff that usually helps me relax — fishing, woodworking, even going to first-aid workshops — I’ve started skipping. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, but because by the time I could do them, I feel like I don’t have the headspace. So yeah, I guess I have been having a harder time relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about your father-in-law that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, there’s a particular memory of him that I’m pushing away or some specific thought that pops up and I have to avoid it.\n\nI’m trying to think about that honestly. It’s not like I’m having flashbacks or replaying one certain conversation with him over and over. Mostly when I think about him, it’s more just the general situation of him being in the house and everything feeling more crowded or tense. But I can’t point to one specific memory and say, “That’s what I’m avoiding.”\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that. If I’m avoiding anything, it’s probably more the overall tension than a specific thought about him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're anxious most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious most of the time, exactly. It’s more like… since my father-in-law moved in, I’m carrying this stress around that I can’t quite put down once I’m home.\n\nAt work, I’m still able to hold it together. Being an EMT, I can kind of switch into that mode where I know what needs doing and I do it. But then I get home, and there’s the situation in the house, and then I’ve got coursework on top of that, and I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I notice I’m shorter with people, less patient.\n\nAnd I’ve started skipping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like going to. So I don’t know if I’d call it being anxious all the time, but I’m definitely more tense and not myself since he moved in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in the things you normally enjoy doing since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, although I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly. It’s more like I still know those things matter to me, but I’m not doing them.\n\nFishing used to be one of the main ways I’d clear my head. Same with woodworking — just having something in front of me where I could focus on measuring, sanding, fixing something with my hands. And I’ve always liked going to first-aid workshops too, even with being an EMT, because it felt useful and kind of grounding in a weird way.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m holding it together at work, and then when I get home there’s all the tension there, and then I’ve got coursework sitting on top of that. By the time I could go do something for myself, I’m already wound up or short-tempered or just mentally done. So I end up skipping it.\n\nSo yes, I’m doing those things less. I don’t think I stopped caring about them, but I’ve definitely pulled back from the things that normally help keep my head clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything — I’m not someone who weighs myself every morning — but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone mention that I look different.\n\nWith all the stress at home, I guess I could see how someone might eat more or less, but I haven’t noticed a big change that way. Maybe my eating is a little less organized some days, just because the house feels off and I’m not always in the mood to sit down for a normal meal, but in terms of actual weight… no, nothing significant that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on edge and can't calm down, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, no. I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there anxious for no reason out of nowhere. It’s more tied to coming home and dealing with the situation there.\n\nLike, at work, I can still function. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on shift I kind of go into that mode where there’s a job to do and I do it. I’m not walking around panicking or unable to calm down. I don’t settle the way I normally would.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help with that — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So it’s not like I’m constantly on edge for no reason, but I’m definitely carrying more stress around at home than feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of home because of your father-in-law's presence?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am, at least more than I used to. I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid of seeing them or anything like that. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in, home has gotten tense enough that I feel like I’m using up all my patience just getting through the day.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together. I’m an EMT, so when I’m there, I’ve got a role and a structure and I just do what needs doing. But then I get home, and there’s the situation with him in the house, and then I’ve still got coursework hanging over me, and I’m just more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nSo when something comes up outside the house — even things I normally like, like fishing, woodworking, or those first-aid workshops I’d usually go to — I end up skipping them. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, exactly. They’re actually the things that usually keep my head clear. But lately I just don’t have the mental room for them, or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're enjoying the things you're still doing, like your work as an EMT, less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I’d put it a little differently. When I’m actually working as an EMT, I can still do the job. I still care about doing it right, and when there’s a call or something needs handling, I can get into that mode and focus. So it’s not like I suddenly hate my work or feel nothing about it.\n\nBut I think I have less room in me, if that makes sense. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to hold it together at work, but then at home — and with my coursework on top of it — I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. So by the time I’m off shift, the things that usually help me feel like myself, like fishing or woodworking or even the first-aid workshops I normally like, I’ve been skipping those.\n\nSo with work, I’m still functioning and I don’t think I’ve lost all enjoyment of it. But I’m not getting as much satisfaction from the rest of my life around it, because I’m just carrying all this stress and not doing the things that usually clear my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed by your emotions most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say most of the time. I don’t feel like I’m falling apart all day long or anything. I’m still holding it together at work, and with EMT work you kind of have to — there’s not a lot of room to get wrapped up in yourself when something needs doing.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in with us, yeah, I’ve felt more overwhelmed than I’m used to, especially once I’m home. It’s like I can keep everything contained during the shift, and then I get back to the house and there’s the tension there, and then I’m also trying to keep up with coursework, and I just feel wound tighter. I get short more easily, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed I’m not doing the things that usually help me reset. Fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — those are usually the things that keep my head clear and make me feel like I’ve got some balance. Lately I’ve been skipping them, which probably makes the whole thing worse. So… not overwhelmed by my emotions every minute, no, but at home it’s definitely been more than I’m used to carrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about your living situation since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it’s not like I’m sitting there every second thinking, “What are we going to do about the house?” It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, it’s kind of become the background noise in my head.\n\nI can still keep it together at work. When I’m on as an EMT, I know what I’m supposed to do, and I can stay focused on the call or the patient or whatever’s in front of me. But once I’m home, or even when I’m trying to do coursework, that tension in the living situation is right there again. I feel more wound up than I’m used to, and I get short more easily, which I don’t like.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me is I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are normally how I clear my head. Lately I just skip them because I feel like I don’t have the space for anything else. So, yeah, I’d say I’m pretty preoccupied with it, especially outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are experiencing anxiety that interferes with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it is interfering, though maybe not in the way people might picture, like I’m completely unable to function. I’m still doing my EMT shifts. When I’m there, I show up, I do the work, and I can stay focused enough to handle what needs handling.\n\nBut outside of that, it’s definitely started to affect things. I’ve fallen behind on some of my paramedic coursework, which bothers me because that’s not really how I usually operate. I’ll sit down meaning to catch up, and then I just don’t have the bandwidth, or I put it off because I feel wound up about everything at home.\n\nAnd I’ve also stopped showing up consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those used to be something I cared about and made time for, and lately I’ve been missing them or not committing the way I normally would. So… yes, I’m still functioning in the big obvious sense, but it’s getting in the way of my daily life, especially with the coursework and the volunteer-type stuff I normally keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific incident or event involving your father-in-law that triggered your feelings of tension and stress?\n\nPatient: Not one specific blowup, no… at least not one I can point to and say, “That was the moment.” It’s more been the whole adjustment since my father-in-law moved in with us. The house just feels different now, and I’ve been trying to keep my head down and keep functioning.\n\nI’m still holding it together at work. When I’m on shift as an EMT, I can focus because I have to. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down with my coursework, I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I get short faster, and that bothers me because I don’t really like being that guy.\n\nAnd I think what made me realize it was getting to me is that I started skipping the things that usually clear my head — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those are kind of my reset buttons, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So no, not a single incident exactly… more the living situation itself has been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about everyday things, like finances or household chores, more often than you used to since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… though it’s not always just one category like finances or chores exactly. It’s more like since my father-in-law moved in, the whole household feels harder to relax in, and little everyday things get under my skin more than they used to.\n\nI mean, I came in because I’ve noticed I can still hold it together at work — when I’m on shift as an EMT, I can stay focused and do what I need to do. But then I get home, or I sit down to work on my coursework, and I’m just more wound up. I’ll be shorter with people than I want to be, or I’ll feel like I can’t settle into anything.\n\nAnd the part that really tells me something is off is that I’ve started skipping the stuff that normally helps me clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are usually the things that keep me balanced, and lately I either don’t go or don’t even make plans for them. So yeah, I’d say I’m worrying and carrying the household stress around more than I used to, even if it’s not always about one specific chore or bill.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think I stopped caring about them, but I’ve definitely pulled back from the things that normally help keep my head clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything — I’m not someone who weighs myself every morning — but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone mention that I look different.\n\nWith all the stress at home, I guess I could see how someone might eat more or less, but I haven’t noticed a big change that way. Maybe my eating is a little less organized some days, just because the house feels off and I’m not always in the mood to sit down for a normal meal, but in terms of actual weight… no, nothing significant that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on edge and can't calm down, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, no. I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there anxious for no reason out of nowhere. It’s more tied to coming home and dealing with the situation there.\n\nLike, at work, I can still function. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on shift I kind of go into that mode where there’s a job to do and I do it. I’m not walking around panicking or unable to calm down. But once I’m home, with my father-in-law living with us now, and then trying to keep up with coursework too, I feel more wound up than I’m used to. I get short faster. I don’t settle the way I normally would.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help with that — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So it’s not like I’m constantly on edge for no reason, but I’m definitely carrying more stress around at home than feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of home because of your father-in-law's presence?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am, at least more than I used to. I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid of seeing them or anything like that. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in, home has gotten tense enough that I feel like I’m using up all my patience just getting through the day.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together. I’m an EMT, so when I’m there, I’ve got a role and a structure and I just do what needs doing. But then I get home, and there’s the situation with him in the house, and then I’ve still got coursework hanging over me, and I’m just more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nSo when something comes up outside the house — even things I normally like, like fishing, woodworking, or those first-aid workshops I’d usually go to — I end up skipping them. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, exactly. They’re actually the things that usually keep my head clear. But lately I just don’t have the mental room for them, or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're enjoying the things you're still doing, like your work as an EMT, less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I’d put it a little differently. When I’m actually working as an EMT, I can still do the job. I still care about doing it right, and when there’s a call or something needs handling, I can get into that mode and focus. So it’s not like I suddenly hate my work or feel nothing about it.\n\nBut I think I have less room in me, if that makes sense. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to hold it together at work, but then at home — and with my coursework on top of it — I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. So by the time I’m off shift, the things that usually help me feel like myself, like fishing or woodworking or even the first-aid workshops I normally like, I’ve been skipping those.\n\nSo with work, I’m still functioning and I don’t think I’ve lost all enjoyment of it. But I’m not getting as much satisfaction from the rest of my life around it, because I’m just carrying all this stress and not doing the things that usually clear my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed by your emotions most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say most of the time. I don’t feel like I’m falling apart all day long or anything. I’m still holding it together at work, and with EMT work you kind of have to — there’s not a lot of room to get wrapped up in yourself when something needs doing.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in with us, yeah, I’ve felt more overwhelmed than I’m used to, especially once I’m home. It’s like I can keep everything contained during the shift, and then I get back to the house and there’s the tension there, and then I’m also trying to keep up with coursework, and I just feel wound tighter. I get short more easily, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed I’m not doing the things that usually help me reset. Fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — those are usually the things that keep my head clear and make me feel like I’ve got some balance. Lately I’ve been skipping them, which probably makes the whole thing worse. So… not overwhelmed by my emotions every minute, no, but at home it’s definitely been more than I’m used to carrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about your living situation since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it’s not like I’m sitting there every second thinking, “What are we going to do about the house?” It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, it’s kind of become the background noise in my head.\n\nI can still keep it together at work. When I’m on as an EMT, I know what I’m supposed to do, and I can stay focused on the call or the patient or whatever’s in front of me. But once I’m home, or even when I’m trying to do coursework, that tension in the living situation is right there again. I feel more wound up than I’m used to, and I get short more easily, which I don’t like.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me is I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are normally how I clear my head. Lately I just skip them because I feel like I don’t have the space for anything else. So, yeah, I’d say I’m pretty preoccupied with it, especially outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are experiencing anxiety that interferes with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it is interfering, though maybe not in the way people might picture, like I’m completely unable to function. I’m still doing my EMT shifts. When I’m there, I show up, I do the work, and I can stay focused enough to handle what needs handling.\n\nBut outside of that, it’s definitely started to affect things. I’ve fallen behind on some of my paramedic coursework, which bothers me because that’s not really how I usually operate. I’ll sit down meaning to catch up, and then I just don’t have the bandwidth, or I put it off because I feel wound up about everything at home.\n\nAnd I’ve also stopped showing up consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those used to be something I cared about and made time for, and lately I’ve been missing them or not committing the way I normally would. So… yes, I’m still functioning in the big obvious sense, but it’s getting in the way of my daily life, especially with the coursework and the volunteer-type stuff I normally keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific incident or event involving your father-in-law that triggered your feelings of tension and stress?\n\nPatient: Not one specific blowup, no… at least not one I can point to and say, “That was the moment.” It’s more been the whole adjustment since my father-in-law moved in with us. The house just feels different now, and I’ve been trying to keep my head down and keep functioning.\n\nI’m still holding it together at work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have noticed some changes, mostly at home. I wouldn’t say I’ve cut people off or had some huge falling-out, but since my father-in-law moved in, I’m just more wound up around the house. I’m shorter than I’m used to being, and I don’t like that. Little interactions feel like they take more effort, and sometimes I hear myself responding in this clipped way and I think, “That’s not really me.”

At work, I can still keep it together. When I’m on shift as an EMT, I sort of know how to compartmentalize and do what needs to be done. But then I come home, or I try to sit down with my coursework, and it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I don’t have the same patience, and I think that affects how I am with family.

With friends, it’s maybe less direct, but I’ve started skipping the things that usually keep me connected and clear-headed — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops I help with. Those are usually the things where I see people, talk, reset a little. Lately I just don’t go or I don’t commit, and then I’m more isolated than I meant to be. So yeah, it’s changed things, not dramatically maybe, but enough that I noticed and that’s part of why I came in.

I guess I’m still trying to sort out exactly what I’m feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m worrying excessively about every little everyday thing, exactly. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m carrying this constant tension around, especially once I’m home. At work, I can still hold it together — as an EMT, I’m used to compartmentalizing and just doing what has to be done.\n\nBut when I get home, and then I’m trying to deal with coursework too, I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I get short more easily, and instead of doing the things that usually clear my head — fishing, woodworking, even first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping them. So it’s not like I’m sitting there worrying about bills or errands or every small thing all day, but the stress around the home situation is definitely spilling into the rest of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have difficulty relaxing since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think “difficulty relaxing” fits pretty well. Restless maybe not in the sense that I’m pacing around constantly, but since my father-in-law moved in, I feel like I’m wound tighter than I used to be, especially at home.\n\nAt work I can still function — I mean, as an EMT you kind of have to stay focused and keep things moving — but once I get home, I don’t really settle. And then I’ve got coursework to deal with too, so it feels like I’m going from one pressure into another without much of a break. I notice I’m shorter than usual, like I don’t have as much patience.\n\nAnd the weird thing is, the stuff that usually helps me relax — fishing, woodworking, even going to first-aid workshops — I’ve started skipping. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, but because by the time I could do them, I feel like I don’t have the headspace. So yeah, I guess I have been having a harder time relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about your father-in-law that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, there’s a particular memory of him that I’m pushing away or some specific thought that pops up and I have to avoid it.\n\nI’m trying to think about that honestly. It’s not like I’m having flashbacks or replaying one certain conversation with him over and over. Mostly when I think about him, it’s more just the general situation of him being in the house and everything feeling more crowded or tense. But I can’t point to one specific memory and say, “That’s what I’m avoiding.”\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that. If I’m avoiding anything, it’s probably more the overall tension than a specific thought about him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're anxious most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious most of the time, exactly. It’s more like… since my father-in-law moved in, I’m carrying this stress around that I can’t quite put down once I’m home.\n\nAt work, I’m still able to hold it together. Being an EMT, I can kind of switch into that mode where I know what needs doing and I do it. But then I get home, and there’s the situation in the house, and then I’ve got coursework on top of that, and I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I notice I’m shorter with people, less patient.\n\nAnd I’ve started skipping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like going to. So I don’t know if I’d call it being anxious all the time, but I’m definitely more tense and not myself since he moved in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in the things you normally enjoy doing since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, although I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly. It’s more like I still know those things matter to me, but I’m not doing them.\n\nFishing used to be one of the main ways I’d clear my head. Same with woodworking — just having something in front of me where I could focus on measuring, sanding, fixing something with my hands. And I’ve always liked going to first-aid workshops too, even with being an EMT, because it felt useful and kind of grounding in a weird way.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m holding it together at work, and then when I get home there’s all the tension there, and then I’ve got coursework sitting on top of that. By the time I could go do something for myself, I’m already wound up or short-tempered or just mentally done. So I end up skipping it.\n\nSo yes, I’m doing those things less. I don’t think I stopped caring about them, but I’ve definitely pulled back from the things that normally help keep my head clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything — I’m not someone who weighs myself every morning — but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone mention that I look different.\n\nWith all the stress at home, I guess I could see how someone might eat more or less, but I haven’t noticed a big change that way. Maybe my eating is a little less organized some days, just because the house feels off and I’m not always in the mood to sit down for a normal meal, but in terms of actual weight… no, nothing significant that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on edge and can't calm down, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, no. I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there anxious for no reason out of nowhere. It’s more tied to coming home and dealing with the situation there.\n\nLike, at work, I can still function. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on shift I kind of go into that mode where there’s a job to do and I do it. I’m not walking around panicking or unable to calm down. I don’t settle the way I normally would.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help with that — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like. So it’s not like I’m constantly on edge for no reason, but I’m definitely carrying more stress around at home than feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of home because of your father-in-law's presence?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am, at least more than I used to. I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid of seeing them or anything like that. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in, home has gotten tense enough that I feel like I’m using up all my patience just getting through the day.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together. I’m an EMT, so when I’m there, I’ve got a role and a structure and I just do what needs doing. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, exactly. They’re actually the things that usually keep my head clear. But lately I just don’t have the mental room for them, or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're enjoying the things you're still doing, like your work as an EMT, less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I’d put it a little differently. When I’m actually working as an EMT, I can still do the job. I still care about doing it right, and when there’s a call or something needs handling, I can get into that mode and focus. So it’s not like I suddenly hate my work or feel nothing about it.\n\nBut I think I have less room in me, if that makes sense. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to hold it together at work, but then at home — and with my coursework on top of it — I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. But I’m not getting as much satisfaction from the rest of my life around it, because I’m just carrying all this stress and not doing the things that usually clear my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed by your emotions most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say most of the time. Fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — those are usually the things that keep my head clear and make me feel like I’ve got some balance. Lately I’ve been skipping them, which probably makes the whole thing worse. So… not overwhelmed by my emotions every minute, no, but at home it’s definitely been more than I’m used to carrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about your living situation since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it’s not like I’m sitting there every second thinking, “What are we going to do about the house?” It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, it’s kind of become the background noise in my head.\n\nI can still keep it together at work. When I’m on as an EMT, I know what I’m supposed to do, and I can stay focused on the call or the patient or whatever’s in front of me. But once I’m home, or even when I’m trying to do coursework, that tension in the living situation is right there again. I feel more wound up than I’m used to, and I get short more easily, which I don’t like.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me is I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are normally how I clear my head. Lately I just skip them because I feel like I don’t have the space for anything else. So, yeah, I’d say I’m pretty preoccupied with it, especially outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are experiencing anxiety that interferes with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it is interfering, though maybe not in the way people might picture, like I’m completely unable to function. I’m still doing my EMT shifts. When I’m there, I show up, I do the work, and I can stay focused enough to handle what needs handling.\n\nBut outside of that, it’s definitely started to affect things. I’ve fallen behind on some of my paramedic coursework, which bothers me because that’s not really how I usually operate. I’ll sit down meaning to catch up, and then I just don’t have the bandwidth, or I put it off because I feel wound up about everything at home.\n\nAnd I’ve also stopped showing up consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those used to be something I cared about and made time for, and lately I’ve been missing them or not committing the way I normally would. So… yes, I’m still functioning in the big obvious sense, but it’s getting in the way of my daily life, especially with the coursework and the volunteer-type stuff I normally keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific incident or event involving your father-in-law that triggered your feelings of tension and stress?\n\nPatient: Not one specific blowup, no… at least not one I can point to and say, “That was the moment.” It’s more been the whole adjustment since my father-in-law moved in with us. The house just feels different now, and I’ve been trying to keep my head down and keep functioning.\n\nI’m still holding it together at work. When I’m on shift as an EMT, I can focus because I have to. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down with my coursework, I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I don’t think I’d call it significantly depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I mean… I’ve definitely been stressed and more weighed down than usual since he moved in, but “depressed all day” feels stronger than what I’ve been experiencing.

There are times I feel pretty low or just worn out by the situation, especially at home, but it’s not like I wake up every morning feeling hopeless and stay that way the whole day. I can still have okay stretches. It’s more that the tension is kind of sitting in the background, and depending on what’s going on in the house, it flares up.

So… no, not most of the day nearly every day. More like I’m strained and not myself as much as I’d like to be.

It’s more like… since my father-in-law moved in, I’m carrying this stress around that I can’t quite put down once I’m home.\n\nAt work, I’m still able to hold it together. Being an EMT, I can kind of switch into that mode where I know what needs doing and I do it.
