It’s more like I still know those things matter to me, but I’m not doing them.\n\nFishing used to be one of the main ways I’d clear my head. Same with woodworking — just having something in front of me where I could focus on measuring, sanding, fixing something with my hands. And I’ve always liked going to first-aid workshops too, even with being an EMT, because it felt useful and kind of grounding in a weird way.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m holding it together at work, and then when I get home there’s all the tension there, and then I’ve got coursework sitting on top of that. By the time I could go do something for myself, I’m already wound up or short-tempered or just mentally done. So I end up skipping it.\n\nSo yes, I’m doing those things less. I don’t think I stopped caring about them, but I’ve definitely pulled back from the things that normally help keep my head clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully or anything — I’m not someone who weighs myself every morning — but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone mention that I look different.\n\nWith all the stress at home, I guess I could see how someone might eat more or less, but I haven’t noticed a big change that way. Maybe my eating is a little less organized some days, just because the house feels off and I’m not always in the mood to sit down for a normal meal, but in terms of actual weight… no, nothing significant that I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on edge and can't calm down, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, no. I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there anxious for no reason out of nowhere. It’s more tied to coming home and dealing with the situation there.\n\nLike, at work, I can still function. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on shift I kind of go into that mode where there’s a job to do and I do it. I’m not walking around panicking or unable to calm down. But once I’m home, with my father-in-law living with us now, and then trying to keep up with coursework too, I feel more wound up than I’m used to. I get short faster. I don’t settle the way I normally would.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help with that — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So it’s not like I’m constantly on edge for no reason, but I’m definitely carrying more stress around at home than feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of home because of your father-in-law's presence?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am, at least more than I used to. I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding people because I’m afraid of seeing them or anything like that. It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in, home has gotten tense enough that I feel like I’m using up all my patience just getting through the day.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together. I’m an EMT, so when I’m there, I’ve got a role and a structure and I just do what needs doing. But then I get home, and there’s the situation with him in the house, and then I’ve still got coursework hanging over me, and I’m just more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nSo when something comes up outside the house — even things I normally like, like fishing, woodworking, or those first-aid workshops I’d usually go to — I end up skipping them. Not because I don’t care about them anymore, exactly. They’re actually the things that usually keep my head clear. But lately I just don’t have the mental room for them, or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're enjoying the things you're still doing, like your work as an EMT, less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I’d put it a little differently. When I’m actually working as an EMT, I can still do the job. I still care about doing it right, and when there’s a call or something needs handling, I can get into that mode and focus. So it’s not like I suddenly hate my work or feel nothing about it.\n\nBut I think I have less room in me, if that makes sense. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to hold it together at work, but then at home — and with my coursework on top of it — I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. So by the time I’m off shift, the things that usually help me feel like myself, like fishing or woodworking or even the first-aid workshops I normally like, I’ve been skipping those.\n\nSo with work, I’m still functioning and I don’t think I’ve lost all enjoyment of it. But I’m not getting as much satisfaction from the rest of my life around it, because I’m just carrying all this stress and not doing the things that usually clear my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed by your emotions most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say most of the time. I don’t feel like I’m falling apart all day long or anything. I’m still holding it together at work, and with EMT work you kind of have to — there’s not a lot of room to get wrapped up in yourself when something needs doing.\n\nBut since my father-in-law moved in with us, yeah, I’ve felt more overwhelmed than I’m used to, especially once I’m home. It’s like I can keep everything contained during the shift, and then I get back to the house and there’s the tension there, and then I’m also trying to keep up with coursework, and I just feel wound tighter. I get short more easily, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed I’m not doing the things that usually help me reset. Fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — those are usually the things that keep my head clear and make me feel like I’ve got some balance. Lately I’ve been skipping them, which probably makes the whole thing worse. So… not overwhelmed by my emotions every minute, no, but at home it’s definitely been more than I’m used to carrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about your living situation since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it’s not like I’m sitting there every second thinking, “What are we going to do about the house?” It’s more that since my father-in-law moved in with us, it’s kind of become the background noise in my head.\n\nI can still keep it together at work. When I’m on as an EMT, I know what I’m supposed to do, and I can stay focused on the call or the patient or whatever’s in front of me. But once I’m home, or even when I’m trying to do coursework, that tension in the living situation is right there again. I feel more wound up than I’m used to, and I get short more easily, which I don’t like.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me is I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are normally how I clear my head. Lately I just skip them because I feel like I don’t have the space for anything else. So, yeah, I’d say I’m pretty preoccupied with it, especially outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are experiencing anxiety that interferes with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it is interfering, though maybe not in the way people might picture, like I’m completely unable to function. I’m still doing my EMT shifts. When I’m there, I show up, I do the work, and I can stay focused enough to handle what needs handling.\n\nBut outside of that, it’s definitely started to affect things. I’ve fallen behind on some of my paramedic coursework, which bothers me because that’s not really how I usually operate. I’ll sit down meaning to catch up, and then I just don’t have the bandwidth, or I put it off because I feel wound up about everything at home.\n\nAnd I’ve also stopped showing up consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those used to be something I cared about and made time for, and lately I’ve been missing them or not committing the way I normally would. So… yes, I’m still functioning in the big obvious sense, but it’s getting in the way of my daily life, especially with the coursework and the volunteer-type stuff I normally keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific incident or event involving your father-in-law that triggered your feelings of tension and stress?\n\nPatient: Not one specific blowup, no… at least not one I can point to and say, “That was the moment.” It’s more been the whole adjustment since my father-in-law moved in with us. The house just feels different now, and I’ve been trying to keep my head down and keep functioning.\n\nI’m still holding it together at work. When I’m on shift as an EMT, I can focus because I have to. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down with my coursework, I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I get short faster, and that bothers me because I don’t really like being that guy.\n\nAnd I think what made me realize it was getting to me is that I started skipping the things that usually clear my head — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those are kind of my reset buttons, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So no, not a single incident exactly… more the living situation itself has been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about everyday things, like finances or household chores, more often than you used to since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… though it’s not always just one category like finances or chores exactly. It’s more like since my father-in-law moved in, the whole household feels harder to relax in, and little everyday things get under my skin more than they used to.\n\nI mean, I came in because I’ve noticed I can still hold it together at work — when I’m on shift as an EMT, I can stay focused and do what I need to do. But then I get home, or I sit down to work on my coursework, and I’m just more wound up. I’ll be shorter with people than I want to be, or I’ll feel like I can’t settle into anything.\n\nAnd the part that really tells me something is off is that I’ve started skipping the stuff that normally helps me clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are usually the things that keep me balanced, and lately I either don’t go or don’t even make plans for them. So yeah, I’d say I’m worrying and carrying the household stress around more than I used to, even if it’s not always about one specific chore or bill.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed some changes, mostly at home. I wouldn’t say I’ve cut people off or had some huge falling-out, but since my father-in-law moved in, I’m just more wound up around the house. I’m shorter than I’m used to being, and I don’t like that. Little interactions feel like they take more effort, and sometimes I hear myself responding in this clipped way and I think, “That’s not really me.”\n\nAt work, I can still keep it together. When I’m on shift as an EMT, I sort of know how to compartmentalize and do what needs to be done. But then I come home, or I try to sit down with my coursework, and it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I don’t have the same patience, and I think that affects how I am with family.\n\nWith friends, it’s maybe less direct, but I’ve started skipping the things that usually keep me connected and clear-headed — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops I help with. Those are usually the things where I see people, talk, reset a little. Lately I just don’t go or I don’t commit, and then I’m more isolated than I meant to be. So yeah, it’s changed things, not dramatically maybe, but enough that I noticed and that’s part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significantly depressed mood for most of the day, nearly every day, since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it significantly depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I’m still doing my EMT shifts. When I’m there, I show up, I do the work, and I can stay focused enough to handle what needs handling.\n\nBut outside of that, it’s definitely started to affect things. I’ve fallen behind on some of my paramedic coursework, which bothers me because that’s not really how I usually operate. I’ll sit down meaning to catch up, and then I just don’t have the bandwidth, or I put it off because I feel wound up about everything at home.\n\nAnd I’ve also stopped showing up consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those used to be something I cared about and made time for, and lately I’ve been missing them or not committing the way I normally would. So… yes, I’m still functioning in the big obvious sense, but it’s getting in the way of my daily life, especially with the coursework and the volunteer-type stuff I normally keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific incident or event involving your father-in-law that triggered your feelings of tension and stress?\n\nPatient: Not one specific blowup, no… at least not one I can point to and say, “That was the moment.” It’s more been the whole adjustment since my father-in-law moved in with us. The house just feels different now, and I’ve been trying to keep my head down and keep functioning.\n\nI’m still holding it together at work. When I’m on shift as an EMT, I can focus because I have to. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down with my coursework, I’m more wound up than I’m used to. I get short faster, and that bothers me because I don’t really like being that guy.\n\nAnd I think what made me realize it was getting to me is that I started skipping the things that usually clear my head — fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I usually help with. Those are kind of my reset buttons, and lately I just haven’t been doing them. So no, not a single incident exactly… more the living situation itself has been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about everyday things, like finances or household chores, more often than you used to since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… though it’s not always just one category like finances or chores exactly. It’s more like since my father-in-law moved in, the whole household feels harder to relax in, and little everyday things get under my skin more than they used to.\n\nI mean, I came in because I’ve noticed I can still hold it together at work — when I’m on shift as an EMT, I can stay focused and do what I need to do. But then I get home, or I sit down to work on my coursework, and I’m just more wound up. I’ll be shorter with people than I want to be, or I’ll feel like I can’t settle into anything.\n\nAnd the part that really tells me something is off is that I’ve started skipping the stuff that normally helps me clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. Those are usually the things that keep me balanced, and lately I either don’t go or don’t even make plans for them. So yeah, I’d say I’m worrying and carrying the household stress around more than I used to, even if it’s not always about one specific chore or bill.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed some changes, mostly at home. I wouldn’t say I’ve cut people off or had some huge falling-out, but since my father-in-law moved in, I’m just more wound up around the house. I’m shorter than I’m used to being, and I don’t like that. Little interactions feel like they take more effort, and sometimes I hear myself responding in this clipped way and I think, “That’s not really me.”\n\nAt work, I can still keep it together. When I’m on shift as an EMT, I sort of know how to compartmentalize and do what needs to be done. But then I come home, or I try to sit down with my coursework, and it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I don’t have the same patience, and I think that affects how I am with family.\n\nWith friends, it’s maybe less direct, but I’ve started skipping the things that usually keep me connected and clear-headed — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops I help with. Those are usually the things where I see people, talk, reset a little. Lately I just don’t go or I don’t commit, and then I’m more isolated than I meant to be. So yeah, it’s changed things, not dramatically maybe, but enough that I noticed and that’s part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significantly depressed mood for most of the day, nearly every day, since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it significantly depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I mean… I’ve definitely been stressed and more weighed down than usual since he moved in, but “depressed all day” feels stronger than what I’ve been experiencing.\n\nThere are times I feel pretty low or just worn out by the situation, especially at home, but it’s not like I wake up every morning feeling hopeless and stay that way the whole day. I can still have okay stretches. It’s more that the tension is kind of sitting in the background, and depending on what’s going on in the house, it flares up.\n\nSo… no, not most of the day nearly every day. More like I’m strained and not myself as much as I’d like to be."
